He tried to buy the founder's clause in the middle of the confession and was told to sit down by a man on some stones. He has not been given anything worse and nothing in this block lands on him, which is the contract's requirement and also the thing he said himself a year ago: that a district is easier to be than a man.** The wire is still in his pocket. |
| **Mara Quill, 34** | **The named person this block makes worse off.** She refused to enter the confession and said why: nine people heard it, one of them is seventeen, and in about four years somebody in a room will ask that boy. **She was asked the question she had been afraid of for seven days and asked it properly, in a yard, and the man of fifty-six said yes on a condition, and she entered the condition before the answer and entered that he asked for it.** She entered no name on the fifty-eighth morning and entered that she was entering a want against nobody but herself. She entered no part of Adrian's page on the sixty-fourth morning: a thing you read in that yard is a conversation. **She told him, on the lane, that a thing you did to somebody is not a thing about you, and that she has been getting that wrong since the fortieth morning and is nearly over, and that she is not going to be one of the things this yard loses. Her no-correction rule is hers at about twenty-five and nine years back; the column's heading is hers at nineteen, on the afternoon she ruled it, and she has still not read it properly.** |
| **Orren Pike, 17** | **He counted a man for the second time in his life, got seventy-six, and published the hole in his own instrument in a yard**, including that he had not been looking at the thing he was asked to look at. He said *I am not going to take seventy off it* and then found six mornings of his own. **He connected the row with *the man* in it to the man at the end of the second table by going back through the six mornings and the third column, and he was asked to strike it and said no because he does not strike things, and was then asked to keep it and say so in a yard and did.** He ruled a third box on the back of his own sheet headed *mornings on which a thing was said out loud in this yard and was not entered anywhere*, copied four mornings into it and ruled a fifth space he left empty. **He is one end of the road keeper's book and not a writer. The man of fifty-six told him in a yard that he put Adrian Vale in the column by asking and has never once asked for himself, and he could not answer it.** He counted the five hundredth morning off a wall and said nothing about it. The wire is still not on his sheet. |
| **Iven Tallow, 61** | **On the page, in that yard, for nine mornings and on the stones on all nine of the nine afternoons, the first of which was the afternoon he arrived.** He wrote the Common Measure; it needed two fresh independent witnesses and a term; he wrote both safeguards in himself; the close came with about nine hundred people in a hall and he could not find two he trusted more than himself and did not go looking long; he took the obligation into himself and left it open until there was somebody better; there has not been one in seventeen years and he has looked twice a year and spoken to about nine thousand people about it. **He has never slept in a room since a morning he can date. He named one thing his own gap does not excuse. He offered Adrian the founder's seat for one private reading in a room, refused to say what the reading would take, attached a condition and refused to name that too, and will not ask again for a year. He told Adrian in advance that in about four years Adrian will be about forty-two and there will be nobody in the room but him.** He has not named the building. He has not ended anything. |
| **the man of fifty-six** | **He refused to read the boy's figure on the fifty-fifth morning and said he had said on the fiftieth morning that he was going to be frightened for about a year, and that he was not going to be asked twice inside a fortnight, and added that he did not say *no*.** He read four figures for the five hundred and fifth time and it was the last of them this block. **He was asked for the column in a yard on the sixtieth morning, asked what happens to the next one, was told nobody knew, and said yes on a condition: a rule about who may be entered, made out loud in a yard in about nineteen people's hearing, before anybody is refused, because a rule written after a refusal is a rule about that person.** He is not in the column and the condition is unmet. **He said the frightening part is not being asked, it is being the only thing in the yard that anybody is sure of, and then read a fifth figure nobody asked him to and said so go on, ask me things.** He has read the four figures out loud for five hundred and five mornings and he still cannot say which one of them is doing something he cannot describe, and he has not been asked again. |
| **the man of about sixty-four** | **On his four hundred and nineteenth night, having slept on four hundred and eighteen of them, and he has been asked two questions in his life and has answered both — by a man at the end of a table on the forty-sixth morning and by a road keeper on the sixty-first — and the one question of his own that anybody will remember is *is it going to end*.** *Is it going to end.* *I don't know.* *Aye.* He refused the eleven seconds and said he would like to keep a figure nobody has ever asked him about. **He told the road keeper things nobody asked him for — a lamp, a cart, four to nine people in a doorway, and a blanket folded at a culvert — and then said out loud that nobody asked him and that every person standing up that bank can now be asked about it and there is not one thing in this basin that can be asked of him and refused.** He asked, once, in front of two people who can be asked, that the reason be entered, both halves or neither. **He did not say no. He still has no line in the lot book, no pay, no household, and a line in a clerk's own book that was entered against his express wish and that nobody in that district has told him about.** |
| **Mavis Dorr, 58** | **THE NAMED COST OF THIS BLOCK.** She told Adrian the price before she said yes and then named nine holders of the district's nine coppers out loud in a yard, and the ninth is Nell Cray, two miles away, on working list forty-one in a corridor nine miles up that hill, who has been said out loud three times in ten mornings and cannot be found by anybody who comes up that lane. **She said *I am not a market, I am a list* in her own voice before anything else, because that is the only difference between a woman who is used and a woman who is doing it.** She said the price first: everybody who hears it can be asked about it afterwards, and six people in that yard were findable that morning who had not been, and one of them is her, and she then named nine more, one of whom cannot be found by anybody who comes up that lane. **She refused Adrian the comfort twice: she would not call the page the first true thing this district has sent anybody, and she would not call it the right answer, because she does not know.** The page went on the boards of the second table with a stone on the corner of it because the only carrier in the district refused it. **A THIRD REVIEW PASS CORRECTED THE CLAUSE THAT FOLLOWED IT IN AN EARLIER VERSION OF THIS FILE, WHICH SAID THE PAGE *IS STILL THERE*. IT IS NOT. `chapter-0780.md:127`, the sixty-fourth morning, records that table bare with the stone lying face up and nothing at either end, and the page is on none of the ten mornings of this block. It is sixteen mornings old and its whereabouts are unstated. What is unchanged is the pressure on it: it is still on no page of hers, and she is still not findable.** **She is still not on any page in this district and still carries for about nineteen people twice a week and has still never sent one of them anywhere.** |
| **the man at the end of the second table** | **About thirty-seven, and still on no page and may not be put on one.** He confirmed in a yard that the row with *the man* in it is his own and that he wrote it through the boy about two years ago. **He asked for it to be struck out, was refused, asked for it to be kept and said so out loud, because a thing asked to be kept is a favour and an instruction has a shape.** He said a hearing is not a question. He told Adrian that what the man of sixty-one wants is not a question, and Adrian has still not conceded it. **He is unpaid, still not the bearer, still refuses to be entered in the column, and the fifth line still has no end on it and was not argued about in this block.** |
| **Tarin Kest, 43** | **He came down that bank for the first time in two years and asked the man on the stones the question he had known the answer to for two years.** He did not accept a concession until Adrian said what followed from it. **His carrier's book is now the only instrument in this basin that can put a fact about a road into a book without a man's name at the top of it, and the boy of seventeen is one end of it and not a writer.** He wrote the entry and read it back to himself and not to the yard. He said the reason he never asked is that asking would have made him responsible and that this is about nine years old and it cost him nothing until about half past three on the sixty-first morning. **The room is unconceded and there is no scene in this volume in which it is conceded; the man at the end of the second table says in Chapter 777 that what Iven wants is a hearing and not a question and that it will look exactly like a question from the top of that bank.** He is the first name ever entered beside the lane count and there is still only one. |
| **the man who drives the cart** | **Still the figure twenty-one, still unnamed, still on no page, and he said NO.** He declined the only page this district has had to send up a lane in a fortnight, out loud, in the ordinary voice, and added nothing to it. His reason: a figure needs a man, and the man would be him, and there is not room in the column for a second name. *I will take a man's page up that lane. I will not take the market's.* **On the sixty-fourth morning he asked for nine words' notice of what is on a page before he carries it, so that anybody who asks him can ask the yard and not the man, and Adrian gave him nine words.** He is still the only carrier in the district. |
| **the woman who keeps a scale** | **She read four lines out of a corridor in a yard on the fifty-third morning of the last block and is on no page and could not be, because the column would not take a second name.** In this block she went to the boards and said that the district has been asking each other what instrument it has for asking a person something, has been told twice that the answer is a yard, and that the first thing the yard is used for is her. **She still wants no name in a book and has said so again. Her catalyst line is the block's engine: the first thing you use it for is not nineteen kitchens and not nine coppers and not a clause nine miles up a hill. It is me.** She has three pages on her own counter and one of them is not hers and there is still no fifth place. She is still unnamed. |
| **the man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally** | The source of the only figure in the district that moves every morning. He said it at a quarter past nine on the sixty-first morning, which is an hour and a half later than the time he entered that morning, and the time he entered was a quarter to eight, which is not the time he said it. He entered no reason for either. He is still unnamed. |
| **Nell Cray** | The person Mavis Dorr carries for. **Named out loud in a yard for the third time in ten mornings, on the fifty-eighth morning, as the ninth of nine holders of the district's nine coppers.** She is on working list forty-one in a corridor in a building nine miles up that hill and the note says ask the market that sent her and this market sent nobody. **She is on no page in this district, she has not been asked about anything, and there is now a page lying on the boards of the second table with nine names on it that were not written on it and her name is one of the nine.** |
| **the man who digs loam** | **About thirty-one and unnamed and not asked anything this block.** He is on the boards at about one on the sixty-second morning watching a boy be told by a man of fifty-six that he put Adrian in the column, and he does not come down that bank any more than he did on the forty-ninth morning. He is not on any page. |

## STILL UNNAMED, AND A SUCCESSOR MAY NOT NAME ANY OF THEM ON THE STRENGTH OF THIS BLOCK

**the man at the end of the second table; the woman who keeps a scale; the man of about twenty-nine who drives the cart; the man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally; the man who digs loam; the turner who comes up that lane twice a day and is not one of Kest's.**

---

# VOLUME 16, BLOCK 0003 — CHARACTER STATE CORRECTED BY THE REVIEW FIX

> **ONE CHARACTER HAD A FIGURE ABOUT HIM PRINTED WRONG FOUR TIMES, IN THREE OF THE VOLUME'S FOUR BIGGEST SPEECHES, BY TWO DIFFERENT SPEAKERS WHO HAD BOTH BEEN TOLD HIS COUNT THAT MORNING. THE FIGURE IS NOT THE INTERESTING PART. WHO KNEW IT AND SAID IT ANYWAY IS.**

- **Adrian Vale.** **His own count in that yard is eighty on the fifty-ninth morning, eighty-one on the sixtieth, and eighty-five on the sixty-fourth, rising one a morning from seventy-six on the fifty-fifth.** In the repaired text Iven Tallow names eighty to his face and Mara Quill names eighty-one, and both of them are people who have heard the boy count him. **Three of the four wrong figures were Iven's, and he was the one man in the yard with the most reason to have it right, and the sentence he used it in — *you have been in this yard eighty mornings and you keep saying we like a man who has not worked out that a district is the thing that cannot be found* — is the sentence that explains Adrian's whole flaw. A figure that small and that wrong does not damage a reader's trust in a book. It damages their trust in a number, and this book has spent thirty chapters asking for the opposite.**
- **Mara Quill.** **The speech of the sixtieth morning is now eight sentences instead of one hundred and twenty words of `and`.** Nothing was taken out of it and nothing was softened: the count of fourteen, the morning nine names were said out loud, the two people he has, the fact that she has never witnessed a term, the boy who has never witnessed anything, and the man of fifty-six who has been read five hundred and one mornings for nothing. **She still says it is a fact and not a feeling and she still says she is the only one of the three of them who does not know what it is worth.**
- **Iven Tallow.** **The repair removes a scene that would have made him better than he is.** He was going to be a man who heard a self-diagnosis and stayed quiet, which is a thing readers forgive a great deal in a sympathetic man and which would have made him passive at exactly the point the volume needs him active. **What he actually does is refuse a sentence and not the man, and say why, at length, twice. That is worse for him and better for the book and it is his.**
- **the man of about sixty-four.** Unchanged by the fix and worth saying anyway: **he has been asked two questions in his life and has answered both, and neither of the two men who asked him wanted nothing.** Nothing in this block made him a person who is easier to talk to. The three people who are easiest to talk to in this yard are all people who have been paid something by somebody.
- **the boy of seventeen.** **His count is the only one in this yard that is a method and not an estimate, and the fix confirms rather than changes it: he is right about Adrian on the fifty-fourth, right about his own hole on the fifty-fifth, right about the row on the sixty-second, and he ruled a box for a figure nobody had asked him to rule a box for.** He is still the only person in that yard who can say *seventy* and *seventy-six* in the same breath without taking either off.
- **A WORD ABOUT WHAT A SUCCESSOR MAY NOT DO WITH THE REPAIRED FIGURES.** **The corrected tenure is not a secret and it is not a reveal.** It is a number four people in a yard now hold and disagree about out loud. A successor who has one of them say it is not repeating the block, and a successor who has a fifth person say it is doing the thing the boy does with his pencil, which is the whole of what the boy is for.


---

# VOLUME 16, BLOCK 0003 — CHARACTER STATE CORRECTED BY THE SECOND REVIEW

> **THE SECOND REVIEW OF THIS BLOCK FOUND FIFTEEN CONTINUITY DEFECTS AND REPAIRED ALL FIFTEEN, AND SIX OF THEM WERE ABOUT THE PEOPLE ABOVE. EVERY ONE WAS A FIGURE OR A RELATIVE DAY IN A MOUTH. NONE WAS A LADDER CELL AND THE LADDER CAME BACK CLEAN.**

- **Adrian Vale.** **Unchanged in the block and unchanged by the repair, and the repair is the point: nothing was moved and no figure about him was softened.** What was wrong was other men's arithmetic about him and one line where he is called thirty-eight in Chapter 775's title and the whole yard's shorthand in the same chapter. He signed a page in his own hand on the sixty-fourth morning and read it out in a yard, and no other person in that block says *Adrian Vale* out loud — Iven Tallow says *Adrian*, at `chapter-0772.md:69`, and got the same answer twice. **Volume 16's stated purpose is to put the name back on him in a scene and the page is a document, so this is on-contract and thin, and the next writer should know that the name is in his own hand and in one mouth and nowhere else.**
- **Mara Quill.** **The two figures that were wrong about her are both corrected: *four days* became *seven days*, twice, and one of the two was in Chapter 776's title.** She said on the lane on the sixtieth morning that a district cannot answer a page and that what she has arrived at is that a district can hold a thing it is not permitted to write down, and that is the first time since the first morning of that book that that yard has produced anything that is not a figure. She entered no part of the confession and she entered the man's condition before his answer and entered that he asked for it, which is a want entered against nobody but herself, and she has now entered three of those in twelve mornings. **She is thirty-four, the figure nineteen is her age on the first entry of a book open for fifteen years, and her no-correction rule is hers at about twenty-five and nine years back.**
- **Orren Pike.** **Unchanged, and the second review confirmed the four places his figure was corrected in the first pass rather than finding a fifth.** He got seventy-six on the fifty-fifth morning by going back through his own page for six mornings he had not been on the boards, and he published the hole in his own instrument in a yard before he knew what it was, and he said *I would like somebody in this yard to hear me say it*. He connected the row with *the man* in it to the man at the end of the second table on the sixty-second morning and was asked to strike it and said no because he does not strike things, and was then asked to keep it and say so in a yard and did. **He is seventeen, the yard says about nineteen, and Sela Pike's bridge charter is sixteen, and those are the two personal spans in that yard and neither is the fifteen or the nine.**
- **Iven Tallow.** **The three figures that were wrong about him are all corrected: *sixteen years* became *seventeen*; *five of the last six afternoons* became *every one of the nine afternoons since he arrived*; and he is no longer described as opening every conversation by naming the parties, the term and what he is taking, which he did once, on the fifty-sixth morning, and has not done since.** He is sixty-one, he has not slept in a room since a morning he can date, he wrote the Common Measure in about nine months to stop anybody owning a bus, a lamp, a ferry, a piece of road or a bell, and he could not find two witnesses he trusted more than himself and did not go looking long and has asked for the looking to be the part examined. **He is on the page for nine mornings, he has not named the building, he has not ended anything, and he has one condition he has refused to name.**
- **the man of fifty-six.** **The figure that was wrong about him is the one that mattered most and it is corrected twice: he does not read four numbers off that wall for *nine years* — his own count is five hundred and two mornings on the sixty-first and five hundred and five on the sixty-fourth, and nine years is the age of the wall — and the figure block no longer says he has been asked by about four people what the oldest of the four means, which no scene established and which he contradicts on the sixty-first morning in his own mouth.** He is still unnamed and may not be named. He said yes to the column and set a condition he has not met, and he is not in the column, and the condition is a rule about who may be entered made out loud before anybody is refused, and nobody in that district can write it.
- **the man of about sixty-four.** **Every claim about him is now arithmetically true and the one that was not has been the most important repair in the block.** Chapter 773 had him saying a road keeper had already asked him what the run is, and on the sixty-first morning the road keeper comes down that bank for the first time in two years and asks him. **He had been asked one question in four hundred and ten nights and has now been asked two and has answered both, and the one question of his own that anybody will remember is *is it going to end*, and Adrian Vale said *I don't know*, and the man said *Aye*, and then refused the consolation and said he has had a figure in a clerk's book about that man for nine years and nobody has ever asked him whether it is right and he would like to keep it.** He is on his four hundred and nineteenth night having slept on four hundred and eighteen of them, he is still not told what is written about him in a clerk's own book, and that is now two blocks old.
- **Mavis Dorr.** **The only figure that was wrong about her is the count of findable people, and it is now one ladder: six before the fifty-eighth morning, nine more named on it, about fourteen by the sixty-third, of whom Iven Tallow says he is the fifteenth.** She named nine holders of the district's nine coppers out loud in a yard and the ninth is Nell Cray, who cannot be found by anybody who comes up that lane. She said *I am a list* in her own voice before she said anything else, and she refused Adrian the comfort twice, and she said out loud that she does not know whether the page is worth anything. **She is fifty-eight, she has never been paid, and nobody in that district knew it was happening.**
- **the man at the end of the second table.** **The one figure wrong about him was his own, and it was wrong in his own mouth in his own chapter: he said *I have been in this yard nine years* where Iven Tallow had said *fifteen years* three mornings earlier and where the block has said fifteen four times. It is now *I have been at the end of this table for fifteen years and I have spent most of them being a line on a book*, and the vague-favour line no longer carries a second tenure.** He is about thirty-seven, he is on no page, a successor may not name him, and he told Adrian on the fifty-ninth morning that what the man of sixty-one wants is not a question but a hearing and that it will look exactly like a question from the top of that bank and is not one.
- **Tarin Kest.** **The figure that was wrong about him was his own tenure and it was wrong four times, in two speeches, in the same chapter, in the middle of the scene the block is built on: *you have been in that yard nine days* twice, against *you have now been at me for thirteen mornings running* in the same chapter. All four are thirteen.** He came down that lane on the forty-eighth morning. He is forty-three. **His carrier's entry is the only instrument in this basin that can put a fact about a road into a book without a man's name at the top of it, the boy of seventeen is one end of it and not a writer, and the room is unconceded on every morning of this volume.**
- **the man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally.** **His figure was wrong in the one direction that a figure block can least afford: Chapter 777 said the count went in at a quarter past nine, which was *nine hours earlier* than it has ever gone in, and it goes in at a quarter to eight. It is an hour and a half later, and the same false figure had been copied into the character state and is now corrected in both files. THE SECOND REPAIR OF IT WAS THE ONE A LADDER CANNOT SEE: the half-hour-and-a-half was still being measured against a habitual quarter to eight that NO CHAPTER GAVE THE MAN, because on the page a quarter to eight is only ever the man of fifty-six reading that wall. THE CHAPTER NOW PUTS THE QUARTER TO EIGHT ON THE PAGE AS WHAT THE MAN ENTERED THAT MORNING, WHICH IS NOT THE TIME HE SAID IT, SO THE INTERVAL IS MEASURED INSIDE THE MORNING AND NO GHOST TIME IS NEEDED.**
- **and the two who are still unnamed and must stay so: the woman who keeps a scale, who has now asked in public for the column and been refused a rule that nobody can write, and the man of about twenty-nine who drives the cart, who said no to a page and yes to nine words.** Neither is on a page. Neither asked to be.

---

# VOLUME 16, BLOCK 0004 — CHARACTER STATE

> **THE TEN CHAPTERS CONTINUED A VOLUME WHOSE ESCALATION WAS ENTIRELY OFF THE PAGE, AND THE PEOPLE BELOW ARE WHAT IS ON IT NOW. TWO `novel-reviewer` PASSES RAN AGAINST THE TEN CHAPTER FILES AND THREE AGAINST THE BLOCK RECORD, AND AN EARLIER DRAFT OF THIS LINE SAID TWO AGAINST THE TEN FILES. THE INVENTORY IS SECTION 6 OF THE BLOCK RECORD. THE HEADLINE FINDING OF THE CHAPTER PASS IS IN ITEM 1 AND IT CHANGED WHO SPEAKS THE LAST SCENE OF THE BLOCK. NOTHING BELOW WAS CHANGED BY THE PASS THAT RAN AGAINST THIS PHASE'S DIFF AFTERWARD, BECAUSE IT FOUND NO DEFECT IN A SINGLE ONE OF THESE TEN CHAPTERS.**

- **Iven Tallow.** **THE LARGEST REPAIR IN THE BLOCK WAS GIVING HIS SPEECH TO THE WRONG MAN, AND IT WAS FOUND BY A REVIEWER, NOT BY THE WRITER.** Chapter 790's closing exchange originally had the man at the end of the second table say *I am sixty-one and I am standing in this yard* and *I said my own name out loud on the fifty-sixth morning* and *I have been the fifteenth person in this district that anybody can look up* — all of which are Iven's, and the man at the end of that table is about thirty-seven and has been at the end of it for fifteen years. **THE PASSAGE WAS REBUILT: Adrian's fourth question is now answered from the stones at the foot of the low wall, in Iven's voice, and the man at the end of the second table says the other half of it into the middle of the yard, to nobody, at the end.** He is sixty-one, he has not slept in a room since a morning he can date, and on the seventy-second morning he read nine lines out loud in a yard in about nineteen people's hearing and gave up a page — which is the better instrument — on purpose, and does not know whether that was brave or the last habit of a man who has been holding something for forty years. **He is on the page, he has not resolved the clause, he has not ended anything, and he has NOT PAID FOR THE CONFESSION. THE CONDITION ON THE SEAT IS STILL UNNAMED AND HE REAFFIRMED THAT IN A YARD, SAID HE WILL SAY NO OUT LOUD IF ANYBODY ASKS HIM AGAIN, AND SAID IT IS THE ONLY THING HE HAS WORTH MORE THAN WHAT WAS ASKED OF HIM.** His fourth reason for not sending the page is the best thing he has said in the volume: he held an obligation for seventeen years on the understanding that it was written down in a form that could be moved, and it is written down, and it cannot be moved, and a boy of seventeen made it immovable, and he did not know that until the morning he said it.
- **the man at the end of the second table.** **About thirty-seven. On no page. May not be named. At the end of that table for fifteen years and a careful man in a yard for nine of them, and the relation between the two figures is stated in this block and not resolved.** **He made the rule that governs the column and he is the first person it refuses, and he is also the man Adrian Vale is named out of.** On the seventy-first morning he described the fifth line out loud in a yard for the first time in fifteen years: a standing offer of his labour with no end on it and no figure against it, four words long, one of them *until*, working every morning since. **He said what it has cost — the ability to want anything, and about nine years of being careful in a yard which he did not notice losing — and then he REFUSED TO LET IT BE THE FIFTH OF THE FIVE**, in front of about nineteen people, and said his own cost is worth exactly nothing to a room nine miles up that hill and is not a debt of this district and not a credit of it. He was asked to be a witness and refused: **a man watching his own handwriting go down is a man with a reason, and a reason is the one thing a witness is not allowed to have.** He is the man who supplied the idea that a thing said out loud in a yard is the only instrument in this basin that cannot be stopped, and he said it was not his idea and had belonged to a man of sixty-one for nine years.
- **Orren Pike.** **Seventeen. THE ONLY PERSON IN THAT YARD WHO COUNTS ADRIAN VALE, AND THIS BLOCK USES THE ONE FINDING THE BRIEF PERMITTED.** On the seventy-third morning he counted Adrian twice in front of a woman with a key, said **ninety-four** on his own page and **ninety-one** on what a person in that yard would count, told her out loud which one was wrong, and **refused to give her the right one.** He said the two figures are different decisions, one about what is true and one about what a person can be asked about afterwards, and that he has never known which is better and that this is the whole of what he is. **His own reason for refusing a witness is the plant this volume's outline has left open since the forty-third morning: there is a row on his page with a word in the first column where a name goes, he noticed it twenty-six mornings back, worked out what to do about it, did not do it and walked away up that lane, and in about four years somebody will ask him what he saw and he saw it and he did not write it down.** He refused to name the man whose row it is, because a row that wants to be a name is settled and worth nothing. He asked a stranger to enter his own refusal, and the clerk refused to enter it because a person in two books cannot get out of either one. **He is now the only person in that district a Council has asked to do a thing and who has refused one, and he said out loud that he worked that out while he was standing there. He has been at the bottom of that bank on six mornings and has said good afternoon to a man on the stones on five of them and not on the second, and he writes the morning in a box and never the thing.**
- **Adrian Vale.** **THE ESCALATION, AND HE LOSES IT IN PUBLIC.** He described a settlement's safety valve in the third person and was caught doing it by the person he was describing it to; he said *then give it a term* in the voice he uses for good ideas and heard the shape of it a second afterwards; he offered to go up the lane alone to the room and read the terms and come back, which is the only version of it he cannot argue with, and was refused on the ground that it is a room with one person in it and a hedge. He was handed the move that would undo the clerk's own sentence in a yard, and did not take it, and the woman who keeps a scale said one word at about forty feet and nobody wrote it down. **His canon flaw did the block's work: on the seventy-third morning he had a wrong figure in his hand that was wrong in his favour, a smaller number makes a man look more replaceable, and it was the first fact in about ninety-four mornings he wanted to put a term on and could not.** He has been in that yard ninety-five mornings and rising one a morning, and has not been given a bed, a wage or an office, has not asked for any of the three, and has a length of fencing wire in his coat about four miles from a gap of a mile and a half that he has not been to and did not go to on the last morning. **He has said neither yes nor no to the seat and has read a clause out loud in a yard without saying what that was.**
- **Mara Quill.** **She is the market of Lot Seventeen and she said so out loud, in a yard, before she had been asked twice.** Thirty-four, the clerk for fifteen years, and the figure nineteen is her age on the first entry of the book. She named herself the fifth of the five: a market without a figure has not decided anything since she was nineteen; a term is a length and this one has no end on it; and there is no person she can pay, and therefore the fifth of the five things this district does not have is her. **She refused to enter the boy's refusal and gave the reason that a person in two books cannot get out of either one, and it is her own rule of the forty-fourth morning turning on her, and it cost the boy his road in the first week it was written.** She read out loud in a yard what was written about a man at the foot of a wall against his express wish, said first that nobody asked her to, entered his asking at his own asking, and read it out before she wrote it. She refused, in the ordinary voice, to say her own refusal in a yard, because the sentence would have Adrian in the middle of it, and asked the boy of seventeen to hear her refuse it. **On the last morning she entered the first thing in fifteen years about that column that is not a name: the one name in it does not satisfy the rule that governs it, and she cannot take it out, and the heading still says *whoever*.**
- **the man of fifty-six.** **HE ASKED FOR A RULE AND THE RULE REFUSED HIM, AND HE DID NOT ARGUE WITH IT.** He is unnamed and may not be named. He is the only person in that district who had been asked for the column and had said yes, and under a rule made out loud in a yard on the sixty-eighth morning he is not in it, because saying yes to being asked is not asking. He said out loud that he knew it would not go his way and asked for it anyway, because what he asked for was not the column but that the next person refused in that yard does not have a reason written about them. **THE WOMAN WHO KEEPS A SCALE TOLD HIM TO HIS FACE THAT IT IS NOT THE FRIGHT AND NOT AN ACCIDENT AND THAT WANTING HAS NOT BEEN ONE OF THE THINGS ON OFFER AT THAT WALL, AND NOBODY HAS GIVEN HIM A COMFORT AND HE DID NOT ASK FOR ONE.** He asked the clerk, in a yard, to enter a sentence she had read out twice, and said he is not asking to be entered: he cannot tell whether he would be able to stand it, and he has had six mornings to find out. **HE WAS NEVER ASKED WHICH OF THE FOUR FIGURES IS WRONG, AND A WOMAN WITH A KEY SAID OUT LOUD, BEFORE SHE ASKED HIM ANYTHING, THAT SHE WAS NOT GOING TO ASK.** He is now a finding in a Council book: five hundred and fifteen mornings of reading four figures twice a morning, never paid, never asked. **NO SPAN IN YEARS, DAYS, MONTHS OR FORTNIGHTS MAY BE GIVEN TO HIM BY ANYBODY IN THIS VOLUME, AND THE FIRST REVIEW OF THIS BLOCK FOUND FOUR PLACES WHERE IT WAS AND ALL FOUR ARE REPAIRED.**
- **the man of about sixty-four.** **THE DEBT TO THE CREDITOR IS PAID AND IT COST HIM.** He has been asked three questions in his life and the third was the clerk's and it was about him. On the seventieth morning he was told, out loud, in a yard, that a page with nothing ruled on it carries a lamp, a cart, four to nine people, a blanket and his reason for standing two hundred yards off in a hedge, entered against his express wish without anybody asking whether it was true or whether he wanted it there, and a figure of eleven seconds that has been in a book for nine years. **He asked one question: does that book say that I asked to keep it. It does not, because it was written before he said it. He said then it is a record of me and not of what I said, and that he has been on a page for about nine mornings and nobody read it to him, and that he has no complaint against the clerk and wants that said before he says anything else.** He asked for the asking to be entered and it was, in her own hand, in the ordinary voice, read out before she wrote it. **He still goes out at about half past four and said so and said it was the only thing about that morning he was able to say out loud and that he had had the whole of the day to think of something better.**
- **the woman of fifty-eight.** **Mavis Dorr, and nothing is paid on her in this block, and she is put at risk twice.** A woman with one of the oldest keys in this basin said out loud in a yard, with no weight on it, *I can find her*, about Nell Cray, and Mavis Dorr said **then find her**, and nobody expected it, and she then said: do not do that as a favour, do it as a Council and not as a kindness, because if you do it as a kindness then in about four years a sentence will have a woman with a key in the middle of it instead of a woman in a market yard. She said in a yard that she is not a market and she is a list, that she said the price before she said the names, and that she does not know what any of it is worth. **THE ASK HAS NOT BEEN MADE. NOTHING IS RESOLVED AND NOBODY IS PAID.**
- **the woman who keeps a scale.** **STILL UNNAMED AND STILL ON NOTHING, AND SHE IS THE ONE WHO WROTE THE RULE THE BLOCK TURNS ON.** She told a man of fifty-six to his face that the reason he is not in that column is not the fright and not an accident. She asked on the sixty-fifth morning for what happened in her yard to be put on a wall, and there is nowhere to put it. She said one word from her own step at about forty feet when Adrian was handed the move that would undo the clerk's own sentence, and the word was *there*, and nobody wrote it down. **SHE IS THE REASON THE FIFTH OF THE FIVE IS NOW ABOUT A PERSON AND NOT ABOUT A PLACE, AND SHE IS NOT IN A COLUMN.**
- **the man of about twenty-nine who drives the cart.** **STILL UNNAMED, STILL THE FIGURE TWENTY-ONE, AND HE SAID THE ONLY UNPROTECTED THING ANYBODY SAID IN TEN MORNINGS.** He says that figure is not room in that yard for a second name of his, and that the figure got into this district on the forty-ninth morning and he did not ask for it and nobody has asked him why. On the last morning, to a woman with a key who was leaving: **you are the first person to come up that lane in nine years who has left without a person, and I did not know until about a week ago that that was a thing that could be done, and I would like somebody in this district to know that I noticed.** She said *I noticed too* and went up the lane. He did not ask to be named.
- **Sabra Holt.** **THE LOCK COUNCIL IS ON THE PAGE FOR THE FIRST TIME IN VOLUME 16 AND IT IS ONE NAMED PERSON AND SHE IS NOT A STRAWMAN.** Fifty-two, nineteen years an appointed administrator, holds the third of the nine oldest keys, and a key is not a seat and a key is not a vote. She came down the lane on the sixty-fifth morning on foot with a page in her right hand. **Her method is paper and her weapon is a rule, a witness, a written record and a signature, and she never once threatened anybody with anything else.** She spent a whole morning asking nothing and said why: a man who reads four figures to whoever stands there is a different man from the one she can get in an hour, and the second would be worth about four words in a room. She refused to make the rule about who may be entered, because a rule of that kind made by a person with a key is a rule a room can take apart with a line of its own, and she has watched four districts learn that in three and be ruined by it in the fourth. She refused to enter the market's sentence. She refused to carry the clause up that hill. She said out loud that a reading is worse than a page and that a room can call a reading a conversation and will be right about the reading and wrong about the clause. **SHE NAMED FOUR THINGS SHE WAS CARRYING UP THAT LANE ON THE LAST MORNING AND THE FIFTH SHE WOULD NOT TAKE, AND THE FIFTH IS A WOMAN ON A WORKING LIST FORTY-ONE, AND SHE SAID SHE WILL ASK HER HERSELF AND NOT THE YARD AND WILL CARRY THE ANSWER UP THE HILL WHETHER IT IS YES OR NO.** She is not resolved and she is on the page.
- **the man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally.** **The interval between the time he says the count and the time he enters it is now stated as a difference and never as an hour, in two chapters of this block, because the two times the pages give him are a quarter to eight and ten o'clock and no other.** He said the count out loud at about ten in front of about nineteen people on the seventy-third morning, with a woman holding one of the oldest keys four feet off him, which is the first time anybody can remember, and entered it at a quarter to eight anyway, and entered no reason for either. **The count is equal to the morning number on all ten mornings of this block and was on all ten of the last.**
- **and the three who are still unnamed and must stay so: the man who digs loam, the man of about thirty-two who puts tables up, and the turner.** None of them has a line in this block. The turner reads whatever is on the wall and is not one of anybody's. The near rail is still not to be thanked for, on ten of ten, and the man who fitted it is not on the page in this block at all.

# VOLUME 16, BLOCK 0005 — CHARACTER STATE AT THE END OF CHAPTER 800

> **THE BLOCK RECORD IS `state/volume-16-batch-0005-summary.md`. `state/character-state.md` IS A RUNNING FILE AND THIS SECTION IS THE STATE AT THE END OF CHAPTER 800, WHICH IS THE LAST CHAPTER OF VOLUME 16. NOTHING IN IT MAY BE A LADDER FIGURE.**

- **Adrian Vale. Thirty-eight. ONE HUNDRED AND FIVE MORNINGS IN THAT YARD, RISING EXACTLY ONE A MORNING, AND IT IS NOT A LADDER. HE HAS NOT BEEN GIVEN A BED, A WAGE OR AN OFFICE AND HAS NOT ASKED FOR ANY OF THE THREE; HE HAS NOT BEEN IN A ROOM NINE MILES UP THAT HILL; THE FENCING WIRE IS STILL IN HIS COAT AND HE HAS NOT BEEN TO THE GAP AND HAS NEITHER SAID YES NOR SAID NO.** In this block he **asked, out loud, in a yard, in about nineteen people's hearing, on his own asking, to be entered in the column for whoever read a thing out loud** — a column which already carries his name, entered on the thirty-ninth morning at the word of a boy of seventeen and not at his own — **and was refused by a clerk whose reason is the sentence of the volume: that the heading over the column is about the reading, the rule is about the asking, and a column filled according to the asking does not answer to the heading over it.** He entered nothing in it and nothing was entered in it. On the eighty-fourth morning, after the man of fifty-six had read the four figures for the five hundred and twenty-fifth time, **he said out loud that everything this basin writes says it is a record of a thing and not of a person, that the founder's clause is a record of a thing that was never a thing with a person's name inside it that nobody in this basin can name, that he has never read it, and that the same thing has been happening at every end of that yard for as long as he has been standing in it — and then he said: a record that cannot name the person it burdens is not protecting anybody, it is only hiding.** He refused the clerk permission to enter it before she had answered, on the ground that a sentence about records entered in a record is a record about a record and its heading would be about a thing. **He is the volume's protagonist and the volume's sentence is his and he gave it away in the open air to nine people and it cannot be carried up a hill.**
- **Iven Tallow. Sixty-one. TWENTY-NINE AFTERNOONS ON THE STONES. THE FOUNDER'S CLAUSE IS UNENDED AND HE IS ITS HOLDER AND HE IS STILL THE MAN IN THE GAP WHERE THE TWO WITNESSES SHOULD HAVE BEEN.** In this block **Sabra Holt asked him in a yard, in about nineteen people's hearing, whether he would stand as the named bearer for the district of Lot Seventeen for a term, and he said no in the ordinary voice, faster than the question, and asked for the asking to be entered before the clerk had written anything.** He gave no reason when asked for one and he did not name the condition on the seat. On the eighty-fourth morning he said, unasked, that it does not matter what happens that day because the only person who can end the clause is him, that he has not ended it and is not going to, and that a room cannot end it because a room is not the holder, and that in about four years somebody is going to come up that lane and offer him a way out of the gap and he has thought about it and is not going to take it. **HE HAS RESOLVED NOTHING, ENDED NOTHING, PAID NOTHING FOR THE CONFESSION, AND THE CONDITION IS UNNAMED AND MAY NOT BE NAMED.**
- **Mara Quill. Thirty-four. THE CLERK OF LOT SEVENTEEN FOR FIFTEEN YEARS.** In this block **she refused Adrian Vale's request to be entered in the column on two grounds and entered his asking with her no attached to it on the page at the back with nothing ruled on it**, which is now the eighth line on a page that has no heading because she ruled it herself. She said out loud that she cannot strike a figure nor replace one and that a column filled according to the asking does not answer to its heading. **ON THE EIGHTY-THIRD MORNING SHE SAID OUT LOUD IN A YARD THAT SHE DOES NOT KNOW WHO RULED THAT COLUMN** — that it was ruled before she was put in front of it, that she entered *not asked* fifty times over the first fifty mornings of it IN A COLUMN FOUR INCHES AWAY ON THE FIRST PAGE WHICH SHE RULED HERSELF, that she wrote the rule about not asking herself, and that she never once carried it those four inches. **THE FIGURE-BLOCK VERSION OF THAT CLAIM — THAT SHE HAD ENTERED A READING UNDER THAT HEADING EVERY MORNING SINCE THAT BOOK WAS OPENED — WAS FALSE AGAINST `chapter-0769.md:88`, WHICH SAYS THE COLUMN WAS EMPTY FOR FIFTEEN YEARS, AND IT WAS REBUILT ON THE PART OF THE BOOK SHE WROTE, WHICH IS TRUE AND IS WORSE.** — **and she refused to enter that finding until the sitting has been, on the ground that she entered something against herself the day before and will not enter two in one day and look gracious. **AND SHE ALSO REFUSED THE MAN AT THE END OF THE SECOND TABLE ON A GROUND THAT WAS FALSE ON THE PAGE AND WAS REBUILT: it said he had read nothing out loud in that yard in fifteen years, and he described the fifth line out loud in that yard on the seventy-first morning, twelve mornings before, and the ground she gives now is that a description is not a reading and the page has nowhere to put the difference.**** She entered one line on the eighty-first morning without reading it out, the first thing she has entered in fifteen years without reading out first. **THE FIGURE OF ELEVEN SECONDS IS STILL ON A PAGE SHE HAS SEEN AND HE HAS NOT.**
- **Sabra Holt. Fifty-two. AN APPOINTED ADMINISTRATOR OF NINETEEN YEARS. THE THIRD OF THE NINE OLDEST KEYS. A KEY IS NOT A SEAT AND A KEY IS NOT A VOTE.** In this block **she refused to carry the page with nine names if it turned up, on the ground that a page that arrives after the yard has already said a name out loud will be believed to have been made first. She asked the man of fifty-six what happens to his wall and told him the truth, which was that a room will use him as the sentence.** She asked Nell Cray in writing, as a Council and not as a favour, and carried the answer up the hill whether it was yes or no; **she said out loud that she chose to carry the worse of two figures about Adrian Vale up that hill rather than the better one, because she cannot withdraw the first.** She refused Adrian Vale's company for the room: a man of thirty-eight walking into it turns a page about the district into a page about himself. **AND ON THE EIGHTY-FIRST MORNING A NOTICE NAMED HER — a woman of fifty-two, nineteen years an administrator, holding the third of nine keys, who states in writing that the district cannot produce a person — AND SHE SAID IN THAT YARD THAT SHE HAS NEVER BEEN NAMED IN A PAGE IN THAT CORRIDOR IN NINETEEN YEARS, THAT SHE DID NOT ASK FOR IT, AND THAT SHE IS NOT GOING TO COMPLAIN, BECAUSE COMPLAINING WOULD SPEND THE ONLY THING SHE HAS EVER WANTED, WHICH IS TO BE FINDABLE.** She went up that lane on the eighty-third morning to say one thing in that room, once, about a piece of paper, and did not say what it was, and **the outcome of that sitting is not on any page of this manuscript.**
- **Mavis Dorr. Fifty-eight.** In this block **she named her own action for what it was when a woman she had spoken for came back and said no in a yard, and said one sentence about having sold something twice without knowing she was selling it.** **HER NINTH NAME, NELL CRAY, IS NOW A WALKED-TWO-MILES NO AND A LINE IN A CLERK'S BOOK. THE PRICE SHE NAMED BEFORE SHE NAMED THEM, WHICH WAS THAT ANYBODY WHO HEARS A NAME SAID IN A YARD CAN BE ASKED ABOUT IT AFTERWARDS, HAS BEEN PAID.**
- **Orren Pike. Seventeen. HIS PAGE IS FOUR YEARS OLD, NOT NINE, AND THAT IS NOW A CANON ENTRY AGAINST `outline/volume-16.md` SECTION 8. HE KEEPS A LIST OF MORNINGS ON WHICH A THING WAS SAID OUT LOUD IN THAT YARD AND WAS NOT ENTERED ANYWHERE, AND HE RULES A SPACE AND LEAVES IT BLANK MOST MORNINGS.** In this block **he said the word *one hundred* out loud in a yard in about nineteen people's hearing when nobody had asked him for it**, having given a woman the wrong figure on purpose six mornings earlier and having had six mornings to decide whether that was about what is true or about what a person can be asked about afterwards, and having decided to find out. He has now caused two irreconcilable figures about one man to exist in two books nine miles apart, and he said out loud that he did not know which of his two decisions was the better one and never had. **ON THE EIGHTY-FOURTH MORNING HE REFUSED TO WRITE THE VOLUME'S SENTENCE IN THE BODY OF HIS SHEET, ON THE GROUND THAT THE DAY THAT SHEET IS FOUND EVERYTHING IN THE BODY OF IT IS A RECORD, AND HE SAID *YOU DO NOT GET TO SPEND MINE EITHER*, WHICH IS THE IDENTICAL SENTENCE A MAN OF THIRTY-EIGHT HAD SAID TO HIM ABOUT A FIGURE FIVE MORNINGS EARLIER AND HAD BEEN TOLD HE COULD NOT.** His own row with a word where a name goes is unchanged and is not to be changed.
- **the man at the end of the second table. About thirty-seven. FIFTEEN YEARS AT THE END OF THAT TABLE AND ABOUT NINE OF THEM CAREFUL IN A YARD, AND THAT SPLIT IS LOAD-BEARING. STILL UNNAMED AND ON NO PAGE.** In this block **he said out loud that he did not know where a page went, that he had seventeen mornings to look for it, that he did not look for it because he did not want it found, and that a page which turns up afterwards is the worst kind there is.** **He asked, standing up, in the light, in about nineteen people's hearing, on his own asking, in the exact words of the rule he made himself fifteen mornings earlier, to be entered in the column, and he satisfied every condition of that rule perfectly, and the clerk refused him because there is nothing in his fifteen years for him to be entered for.** He did not argue. **He said *then the rule is not the problem*, that the problem is a heading, that a heading is not a figure and nobody in this basin can strike a line under a heading — and then he asked who ruled that column, and a clerk who has been clerk for fifteen years said twice that she does not know.** He said two men have been refused out of that room inside seven mornings and neither refusal was made by a person, and that in about four years somebody is going to put the two entries side by side and conclude that this district refuses everything it is asked. **He declined, out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, to ask Adrian Vale what the fencing wire is for, and said he would keep not asking.**
- **the man of fifty-six. FIFTY-SIX. FIVE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-FIVE MORNINGS OF READING FOUR FIGURES OFF THAT WALL TWICE A MORNING, AND NOBODY HAS EVER PAID HIM AND HE HAS NEVER ASKED. STILL UNNAMED. MAY NOT BE GIVEN A SPAN IN YEARS, DAYS, MONTHS OR FORTNIGHTS.** In this block **he asked one question out loud in a yard for the first time in five hundred and sixteen mornings — what happens to that wall — and asked for the asking to be entered, and it was.** He was told that a room would use him as the sentence, and he said he would like it entered that he asked and what he asked and that she told him the truth and it was not a good answer, and that whatever happens to that wall it was his for every morning of it and nobody took it off him, and he could not say thank you that would mean anything and said that instead. **He read the four figures to one man who asked for them, once, at about nine feet of yard, and when a woman with one of the oldest keys ruled that it was not to happen twice he asked for it entered that it happened once and that he would do it again, and she entered it.** He was never asked which of the four is wrong and nobody in that yard proposed to ask him.
- **the woman who keeps a scale. UNNAMED AND ON NO PAGE. NINE YEARS ON HER OWN STEP ABOUT FORTY FEET OFF THE FOOT OF THAT BANK.** **She carried the scale up eleven feet of bank in both hands for the first time in nine years, let nobody touch it, and said out loud that she has had nine years of other people's weight across that beam and has never once put a person on it, because there is no figure in this district that a person can be weighed against, and that this is the fifth of the five and has been since she was forty and she never said it out loud because a thing said out loud in that yard gets used.** She named four people in that yard who can be named and cannot be paid and said she is the fifth of them. She asked Adrian Vale what he is paid in and would not leave the answer alone. **She refused him her step in front of about nineteen people and said why: a man who comes to a step is a man being photographed giving something to a person who has nothing, and she has been in about four of those in nine years.** She asked him whether he would be here in ten mornings; he said he did not know; she said that has been the answer off that step every morning for nine years and has never once changed, and that this morning it was the same answer and she has got a different figure out of it and will not say what it was. She went back down the bank with the scale and nobody followed her.
- **the turner. About forty-one. HE HAS COME UP THAT LANE TWICE A DAY FOR NINE YEARS AND HAS BEEN ASKED NOTHING BY ANYBODY IN ANY OF THEM, AND HE READS THAT WALL OFF THE WALL IN A LANE WITH NOBODY IN IT. STILL UNNAMED AND NOT ONE OF ANYBODY'S.** **He asked the man of fifty-six to say the four figures to him and gave the reason out loud in a yard: he wanted to know whether he had been wrong for six years and he is not going to find out.** The man of fifty-six said them. A woman with one of the oldest keys in this basin ruled that it was not to happen twice. **He said he is going on reading them off the wall, that not being asked is not the same as having nothing to say, that he has said nothing to that yard for nine years because it was cheaper and has got about nine coppers' worth of nothing out of it, and that he answered every question she asked and asked for none of them back.** He carried a figure of twenty-one that belongs to the man who drives the cart and has no figure of his own.
- **the man who drives the cart. About twenty-nine. STILL THE FIGURE TWENTY-ONE, STILL UNNAMED, STILL NOT ON A PAGE, AND HE DID NOT ASK TO BE.** In this block he broke seven mornings of silence on the eighty-first morning and put the sentence that keeps Sabra Holt findable into her own mouth as a choice: **you could complain about it, and complaining costs more, because complaining spends findability.** She answered it and it was hers to refuse. **AN EARLIER DRAFT OF THIS BLOCK'S RECORD GAVE THAT SPEECH TO HER AND THE PAGE GIVES IT TO HIM, AND THE RECORD HAS BEEN CORRECTED.**
- **Nell Cray. THE SIXTH OF THE NINE COPPERS. NINE YEARS ON WORKING LIST FORTY-ONE IN A CORRIDOR NINE MILES UP THAT HILL AND TWO MILES FROM THAT YARD. NAMED OUT LOUD ON THE FIFTY-EIGHTH MORNING AND NEVER ASKED ABOUT ANYTHING BY ANYBODY UNTIL A WOMAN WITH A KEY ASKED HER IN WRITING AS A COUNCIL.** **She walked two miles on foot and said no out loud in a yard in about nineteen people's hearing, and said it in the sun because a sentence in a letter is a thing you can hand round a room and nobody in it has to look at your face while they read it. She said she will not stand in a room for a term, and that a list has paid for everything she has ever done and she is not going to have her name be a second thing at the bottom of her. She said the hard half: that this district does not have nine holders of nine coppers, that the nine of them are on a list that holds them, and that every one of the nine is already paid by something with no figures and no end on it, and that nobody has ever asked any of the nine whether that counts.** Her no and her reason are entered in a clerk's own hand in that district and are on no page nine miles up that hill.
- **the man of about sixty-four. ON HIS FOUR HUNDRED AND THIRTY-NINTH NIGHT OF THAT RUN, HAVING SLEPT ON FOUR HUNDRED AND THIRTY-EIGHT OF THEM. HE HAS BEEN ON A WALL FOR NINE YEARS, GOES OUT AT ABOUT HALF PAST FOUR, IS PAID NOTHING, AND HAS ASKED NO MORE QUESTIONS THAN A PERSON MAY REASONABLY ASK.** In this block **he said good afternoon to a man of sixty-one for twenty-nine consecutive afternoons and on the last one he said, unasked, that he had been four hundred and thirty-nine nights and that it is nine miles up that hill where he is going and there would be carts on that road at about five and he was not going to stop and he was not going to be looked at, and that he had gone past a building where a room was deciding something about a district of about nineteen people on a great many mornings and nobody has ever asked him why and he would have said.** He went out at about half past four on the morning that room sits and this manuscript does not say when he came back.
- **and the three who are still unnamed and must stay so: the man who digs loam, the man of about thirty-two who puts tables up, and the road keeper who keeps every road in this basin. The man of about thirty-two took a stone off the second table on the seventy-fifth morning and carried it down the bank inside his coat and refused to say where he put it, and said a hollow with nothing in it is a sentence. The near rail is still not to be thanked for, on ten of ten.**
